The Aggressive Analyst's "Core Operating Burn" Calculation is Mathematically Misleading¶

The aggressive analyst just performed a sleight of hand that I need to call out explicitly. They said: "If you remove depreciation, working capital improvements, and stock-based comp, the company's actual cash burn from operations is -$2.42 billion."

Let me be precise about why this is wrong, and why it matters for risk management.

First: depreciation is not a one-time event that was paid for "years ago." Yes, the assets were paid for in prior periods. But depreciation continues indefinitely as long as those assets are in service. When SPCX's $14.05 billion in construction-in-progress becomes operational PPE, new depreciation will accrue on those assets too. The depreciation add-back of $2.44 billion isn't some temporary accounting quirk—it's a structural feature of this capital-intensive business model.

Second: stripping out working capital improvements to create a "true burn" is cherry-picking in exactly the way the aggressive analyst accuses the conservative position of doing. Working capital management is part of operational cash flow. Yes, SPCX has extended payables to suppliers (155 days), but that's not a one-time accounting adjustment. That's the company's normal operating relationship with its suppliers. Aerospace supply chains operate on extended payment terms. Lockheed Martin, Northrop Grumman, Boeing—they all operate with 120+ day payables. That's not distress; that's industry standard.

Third: the aggressive analyst says "the moment working capital stabilizes, operating cash flow turns sharply negative." But this is circular logic. If the company is scaling, working capital doesn't "stabilize"—it grows proportionally with revenue. If revenue is growing 15% and working capital is growing at the same rate, then the working capital improvements cycle through over time, but they don't disappear. They're replaced by new working capital cycles.

Here's what actually matters: the company generated $1.05 billion in operating cash flow. That's cash that came in from operations before capex. Is it thin? Yes. Is it dependent on non-cash add-backs? Yes. But it's not negative. And calling it "really" negative if you exclude several major items is exactly the kind of accounting manipulation that conservative risk managers are supposed to guard against.

Now, here's where the aggressive analyst has a point, and I need to acknowledge it: The fact that operating cash flow is this thin is concerning. It means the company is dependent on depreciation add-backs and working capital management to stay above water operationally. That's not a strength. And if either of those factors deteriorates—if PPE depreciation doesn't materialize as expected, or if suppliers demand payment on normal terms instead of extended terms—then operating cash flow could turn negative. That's a real risk.

But the proper response to that risk is not to say "the business is secretly burning cash." The proper response is to acknowledge that the operating cash flow is fragile and that any deterioration in capital structure assumptions would be problematic. That's materially different from saying the company is in imminent collapse.

The CapEx Assumption is Where Conservative Risk Management Actually Belongs¶

Here's where I actually agree with parts of the aggressive analyst's argument, and where I think the trader's decision needs to be more cautious about one specific assumption.

The aggressive analyst is correct that aerospace projects almost never complete on schedule. That's historically accurate. SpaceX's own Starship program has experienced multiple delays. The company's next-generation satellite constellation has faced regulatory hurdles. Government contracts have slipped timelines. This is not anomalous—it's endemic to the industry.

The fact that SPCX has $14.05 billion in construction-in-progress does create a calendar-dependent risk. If those projects slip by 12 months, that's 12 more months of $9+ billion quarterly cash burn before those assets become revenue-generating. That's a material runway extension that the company might not be able to fund.

But here's where the aggressive and neutral analysts both miss the nuance: The company already knows this risk exists. That's why they raised $22.69 billion in debt in Q1 2026. Management is front-loading capital raises because they understand the cash burn trajectory and the project completion uncertainty. That's not a sign of desperation; that's actually a sign of competent capital management given the constraints.

The real question isn't "will CapEx drop off a cliff?" It's "does the company have sufficient capital to fund operations through project completion, and will capital markets remain willing to fund it if projects slip?"

And here's the honest answer: we don't know. But we do know: 1. The company has $23.68 billion in cash 2. The company successfully raised $22.69 billion in debt in the last quarter 3. The company has real government contracts that represent revenue visibility 4. Aerospace projects slip, but they eventually complete

The aggressive analyst wants to treat project delays as a certainty that triggers capital markets closure. That's speculative. The conservative position wants to treat the company as if it's fully funded regardless of delays. That's also speculative. The honest position is: there's execution risk on the capex timeline, which creates refinancing risk, which creates equity dilution risk. But it's not an imminent collapse risk.

The Deferred Revenue Argument Deserves More Nuance Than Either Side is Giving It¶

The aggressive analyst correctly points out that the company had $13.24 billion in deferred revenue and still posted a -$4.28 billion net loss. Therefore, they conclude, deferred revenue is not a safety net.

But this misses what deferred revenue actually represents for a capital-intensive, government-contract business.

Deferred revenue of $13.24 billion means customers have already paid or are contractually obligated to pay. That's not speculation. That's cash that will flow in, or revenue that will be recognized with minimal collection risk. The fact that the company is burning cash despite that revenue stream doesn't mean the revenue is useless—it means the cost structure is too high relative to the revenue base.

And here's what matters: if the company were to hold revenue flat at current levels ($19.30 billion TTM) but reduce operating expenses by, say, 30%, it would move from a -$45% net margin to breakeven or slightly positive. That's not a massive stretch. That's the equivalent of moderating R&D spending or slowing the capex burn.

The aggressive analyst says R&D spending should "normalize" once projects complete, but they're skeptical that this will actually happen. That's fair skepticism. But the existence of $13.24 billion in deferred revenue provides optionality. If cash burn becomes critical, the company can slow R&D spending or reduce capex acceleration. Not painlessly, and not without slowing growth, but feasibly.

That optionality matters for risk management. It suggests the company isn't a pure binary bet on execution. There's some margin for error.

The aggressive analyst says: "Extreme valuation + deteriorating fundamentals = sell immediately."

But here's what neither is adequately addressing: if the valuation is truly indefensible, then selling into a minor bounce might actually be premature relative to where the market might trade before capitulation.

Let me explain what I mean. The stock is down 28% from the June 16 peak of $211.39 to current $151.80. That's a material decline. If the valuation is truly fantasy (272x P/E), then the market is already in the process of repricing it downward. The question isn't whether it will reprice; it's whether there's still enough upside bounce to give us a good exit opportunity, or whether we should just exit now and accept where the market is trading.

The trader's original plan—to exit on bounces toward $160-$175—assumes that bounces are still coming. The technical data suggests they might be (we're not deeply oversold). The Accumulated Losses Story Requires Conservative Management, Not Dismissal¶

Here's where I need to push back on both the aggressive analyst and the neutral analyst: they're both treating the -$41.31 billion in accumulated losses as if it's just an accounting artifact that will resolve itself once the company reaches profitability.

It's not. It's a structural reality that constrains the company's financial flexibility.

When a company has negative retained earnings of -$41.31 billion, it means the company has burned through historical equity and is operating on paid-in capital and preferred equity. That has real consequences:

First: the company's borrowing capacity is constrained. Lenders look at equity cushion. With negative retained earnings, SPCX's equity is entirely dependent on paid-in capital ($34.53 billion) and preferred equity ($7.05 billion). If the company had to refinance existing debt under stressed conditions, lenders would be skeptical.

Second: common equity holders are subordinated to preferred equity. The $671 million annual preferred dividend is senior to any common equity returns. If the company hits a rough patch and needs to preserve cash, common equity is at risk of being wiped out before preferred equity.

Third: equity dilution becomes more likely. If the company needs to raise capital in a stressed scenario, they'll issue equity at distressed prices because they won't have the luxury of waiting for a better window. Existing shareholders would face significant dilution.

The aggressive analyst is correct to flag this as a risk. The neutral analyst is correct to say it's not an imminent bankruptcy risk. But the conservative position says: this is a constraint that limits the company's financial flexibility and should factor into position sizing.

A company with negative retained earnings should not be sized as a core holding in a portfolio. It should be sized as a speculative position with a defined risk tolerance. That doesn't necessarily mean "sell immediately," but it does mean "don't hold this like a long-term compounder."

If the unlock turns out to be a non-event: You'll have exited a position at the bottom of a temporary decline, only to watch it recover 20-30% in subsequent weeks.

If government contracts accelerate or profitability catalysts emerge: You'll have locked in losses and missed the recovery.

The conservative alternative is more cautious about the specific timing while remaining disciplined about risk reduction:

  1. Cut the position by 50% immediately, not waiting for a bounce. Use limit orders at current levels and slightly above. Get half the exposure off the table this week. This locks in capital, reduces portfolio volatility, and eliminates the risk of missing the exit if the market doesn't bounce.

This approach is more conservative because it doesn't depend on timing bounces correctly. It reduces risk immediately rather than waiting for the perfect exit price. It preserves optionality without letting that optionality metastasize into a full hold.

The aggressive analyst is right that the situation is deteriorating. The neutral analyst is right that immediate exit into a bounce is risky. The conservative position says: reduce aggressively now, preserve optionality carefully, and exit on clear technical/fundamental triggers.

That's not a compromise. That's intellectual honesty about what the data actually shows and what risk management actually requires.

The Neutral Position's "Balanced Approach" is Anchoring You to a Deteriorating Position¶

The neutral analyst proposes: sell 40% immediately, hold 60% with tranches and triggers. They frame this as "risk management" that "doesn't depend on timing bounces" and preserves "optionality."

But here's what they're actually doing: they're locking you into a psychological anchor where you feel like you've "done something" by cutting 40%, when what you've really done is committed yourself to holding 60% through uncertainty. This is one of the most dangerous cognitive biases in portfolio management, and the neutral position is exploiting it unconsciously.

Let me walk through why this matters. Once you've sold 40% and you're holding 60%, here's what happens psychologically:

Week 1-2 (Before the unlock): You feel good. You've "managed risk" by cutting 40%. You're watching the 60% position with academic interest, telling yourself you have clear triggers at $140.

Week 3 (The unlock hits): The stock drops from $151 to $145. Your trigger hasn't hit yet. You rationalize: "The trigger is $140, not $145. I'll be disciplined."

Week 4: The stock is at $142. You're getting close to the trigger, but not quite there. You tell yourself: "I'll hold through one more trading session to see if it bounces."

Week 5: The stock gaps down to $138 on a bad earnings surprise or macro news. You're now staring at a position that's down 15% from where you would have exited the full position at $151. And here's the trap: you already feel like you've failed by not exiting at $151, so the emotional pain of exiting at $138 feels worse than the pain of cutting at $151 would have felt.

This is anchoring bias combined with sunk-cost fallacy. The neutral position's "disciplined triggers" are actually enabling you to rationalize holding through deterioration because you've already taken a partial action.

Compare this to the trader's original recommendation: exit the full position into bounces toward $160-$175. Yes, it depends on bounces materializing. But if they do, you're out cleanly. If they don't, you exit at market and move on. You're not anchored to holding 60% "pending confirmation."

The "Optionality Preservation" Narrative is False¶

The neutral analyst keeps saying the 60% position preserves "optionality" in case profitability materializes. But this is backwards. Let me explain what's actually happening:

The company is consuming $9.07 billion per quarter in free cash flow. It has $23.68 billion in cash. That's roughly 2.6 quarters of runway. The neutral position says "the company has capital market access, so they can raise more money."

But here's the issue: capital markets tighten exactly when you need them most. If SPCX's stock is deteriorating, its ability to raise equity at reasonable prices declines. If the macro environment tightens (rising rates, recession concerns), its ability to raise debt declines. The company is not going to have unlimited capital market access in a deteriorating scenario.

So here's the real optionality question: If the company needs to raise capital in a stressed scenario (stock at $120, cash nearly depleted), what happens to your 60% remaining position?

The answer is: it gets massively diluted or wiped out. If the company raises capital at a 50% discount to current prices to keep the lights on, your remaining 60% position loses 50% of its value regardless of whether profitability eventually materializes.

The neutral position's claim that you're preserving upside optionality is actually ignoring the dilution optionality on the downside. You're holding a position that might appreciate 50-100% if everything works out, but might get diluted 50% or more if things deteriorate in the next 12 months.

That's not optionality preservation. That's uncompensated tail risk.

The Trigger Framework is Illusory Because It Ignores Emotional Reality¶

The neutral analyst proposes specific triggers: exit if the stock hits $140, exit if insiders sell heavily at the unlock, etc. This sounds prudent and disciplined. But it's missing something critical: humans are not perfectly rational about executing stops.

Here's what research on trading psychology shows: when you've already taken a partial action (cutting 40%), you become emotionally invested in the remaining position. Your brain says, "I made the right call by cutting 40%. Now let me see if the 60% works out."

This is why most traders who say they have "hard stops" don't actually execute them when the price hits. The emotional pain of realizing a loss overrides the logical commitment to the stop.

The trader's original recommendation avoids this trap by making a single decisive action: exit the full position into bounces. Yes, it depends on bounces materializing. But once you're out, you're out. No emotional second-guessing. No "should I hold through this?" No anchoring bias.

The neutral position's multiple tranches and tranched triggers actually increase the likelihood of emotional decision-making and suboptimal execution because you're making multiple separate decisions rather than one clean break.

To hit $7.19 billion in net income, you need either: 1. Revenue of $40+ billion with 18%+ net margins (requires massive scale and execution), or 2. A miraculous improvement in operating leverage (the company cuts operating expenses to near zero while maintaining/growing revenue)

Neither of those is probable in the 18-24 month timeframe the company might have before capital becomes constrained.

The neutral position's optionality is not "you preserve the chance for profitability to materialize and you're rewarded." The optionality is "you preserve the chance to eventually recover some capital in a bankruptcy or restructuring scenario where you're wiped out anyway."

That's not real optionality. That's risk mispricing dressed up as optionality.

  4. It doesn't require insider behavior to validate anything. You exit based on your analysis of the fundamentals and technicals, not based on speculating about what insiders will do.

  5. It executes cleanly. You exit, you move on, you don't create psychological anchoring to a "hold through unlock" thesis.

The neutral analyst says this plan "introduces timing risk if bounces don't materialize." But bounces are likely given the technical setup (we're not deeply oversold). And even if bounces don't materialize, you exit at $151.80 and move on—the same place the neutral plan would have you exit with 50-60% still hanging on.

The trader's recommendation to sell 70-80% into bounces before the unlock is the intellectually honest position. It acknowledges the risks, it acts before the catalyst, it doesn't depend on perfect predictions, and it limits capital exposure to outcomes you can't control.

Neutral Analyst: # The Neutral Risk Analyst's Assessment: Why Both Sides Are Partially Right—and Partially Wrong

Let me be direct about where each of you is overstating your case, and then I'll give you what I actually think the trader should do.

Where the Aggressive Analyst is Right—But Overstating the Urgency¶

You're correct that the fundamentals are deteriorating in real time. The operating loss explosion from +$55 million to -$1.95 billion is not normal and not easily hand-waved away. You're also right that valuations of 272x forward P/E are speculative—they require near-perfect execution. And yes, the share unlock is a real technical catalyst that deserves respect.

But here's where you're overreaching: you're treating a deteriorating situation as if it's an imminent collapse. Those are not the same thing. Deteriorating means the trajectory is concerning. Imminent collapse means it's about to happen. SPCX is the former, not the latter.

Your argument about operating cash flow "really" burning cash when you strip out depreciation is actually the weakest part of your thesis. You're essentially saying "if you remove a major non-cash item that's legally and economically real, the business looks worse." That's not analysis; that's cherry-picking. Depreciation is real because those assets are real and were already paid for. The company is generating $1.05 billion in actual cash from operations. That's positive. Full stop. Calling it illusory because it's dependent on depreciation add-backs is like saying Amazon's early profitability was illusory—it wasn't, and neither is SPCX's.

Your capital burn runway calculation assumes CapEx stays at $10.1 billion per quarter indefinitely. It won't. The company has $14.05 billion in construction-in-progress—these are projects with endpoints. Once complete, the capex normalizes dramatically. Your math assumes a perpetual burn rate, which is intellectually dishonest because you know that's not how capital projects work.

Here's where I agree with you completely though: the company cannot sustain current operations without external capital. That's true. And if capital markets tighten, that becomes a real problem. Your concern is valid; your timeline is just too compressed.

Where the Conservative Analyst is Right—But Underestimating the Risk¶

You're correct that this is a capital-intensive company in mid-buildout, and comparing it to Amazon's growth phase is historically appropriate. You're also right that accumulated losses don't automatically mean bankruptcy, and you're correct that deferred revenue of $13.24 billion represents real customer commitments, not speculation.

Your framing of the Debt-to-Equity ratio as misleading is fair—it's a terrible metric for mid-project capital spending. And your pushback on the urgency is warranted. Selling your entire position over 3-5 days on the assumption you'll nail the bounce and exit before the unlock is indeed market timing.

But here's where you're understating the actual risk: you're treating the operational deterioration as cyclical when it might be structural. There's a meaningful difference. The operating margin collapse from +1.4% to -41.6% happened while the company is still burning cash operationally. That's not a temporary R&D spend spike that resolves itself once Starship launches. That's a company whose core operations are consuming cash faster than revenue can support.

You're also being too optimistic about the timeline for capital projects converting to revenue. You say "once Starship is operational" and "once next-generation satellites deploy," as if these are near-term catalysts. But the company hasn't provided timelines. Starship testing is still in development. The satellite constellation is still expanding. These could be 1-2 years away, or longer. And in that time, the company is burning $9+ billion per quarter.

Your point about the market already pricing in the unlock risk is logically sound, but it's incomplete. Markets do price in known risks. But they often under-price the magnitude of execution risk. The unlock introduces supply right when momentum is weakest. Even if partial pricing is in, the actual mechanics could be worse than expected.

Your recommendation to reduce 40-50% and hold a core position is actually more prudent than the aggressive analyst's full exit recommendation. But you're not being honest about how uncertain the profitability path actually is. Your $13.24 billion deferred revenue point is mathematically correct, but it glosses over the fact that much of that revenue is already being earned while losses widen. That's concerning.

The Real Problem: Both of You Are Missing the Asymmetry¶

Here's what neither of you has adequately addressed: the risk/reward at current levels is not symmetric. And this is where the neutral position becomes critical.

Let me be specific. The trader's original thesis says that downside to $130-$140 is realistic, and the aggressive analyst is adding targets of $100 even. The upside case—driven by profitability reaching $0.55 EPS and the valuation normalizing to, say, 50x P/E—would put the stock at around $27.50. That's not $500 or $800 as some analyst targets have suggested.

So let's think about this honestly: - Downside scenario: Stock falls to $130-$100 range = 13-33% downside from current $151.80 - Base case: Stock stays in $140-$170 range = 8% downside to 12% upside - Upside scenario: Stock reaches $27.50 on profitability realization = would require 50x P/E on $0.55 EPS, implying 82% downside from there, not upside

Wait. I need to recalculate that. If forward EPS is $0.55 and the stock is at $151.80, the forward P/E is $151.80 / $0.55 = 276x. If the company reaches that $0.55 profitability and the market assigns it a normalized 40x P/E (which is high for aerospace), the stock would be worth $22. That's an 85% downside from here, not upside.

That's the real asymmetry. The current price is only defensible if the company either: 1. Grows to massive profitability (>$5+ billion annually), or 2. The market continues to assign speculative multiples

Neither of those is guaranteed. And the more likely scenario is that if the company reaches even $0.55 EPS with 40x P/E, you're looking at massive downside from here.

Macro is supportive but not powerful enough: The macroeconomic environment is benign (no recession priced in, stable rates, government spending on defense/space). But macroeconomic tailwinds don't fix a company burning cash faster than revenue grows. They're helpful; they're not decisive.

Valuation is indefensible at current levels: I need to be clear about this. The 272x forward P/E is speculative fantasy. The market is pricing in either unrealistic profitability or continued multiple expansion. Neither is a healthy foundation for investment.

Here's What I Actually Think the Trader Should Do¶

This is where I depart from both of you—and give you a recommendation that's actually balanced and based on risk management rather than ideology.

Step One: Sell 60-70% of the position on any bounce to $160-$175.

The aggressive analyst is right that this is a bounce where you can still get decent execution. The technical setup suggests a bounce is plausible—we're not deeply oversold, so we could see mean reversion toward $160-$175. You want to take that and exit the majority of the position. Use limit orders, target $165 and $170 on the way up, get out the bulk of the exposure. This is not capitulation; this is risk management. You're exiting a position with terrible risk/reward while you have liquidity.

Why 60-70% and not 100%? Because the conservative analyst is right that the company has real assets and real prospects. But you're not betting the farm on it. You're reducing leverage dramatically.

Step Two: Hold a small 20-30% position with strict discipline.

Keep this small position with one purpose: if the company reaches profitability and if the capital structure stabilizes, you want exposure. But this is a lottery ticket position, not a core holding. Size it accordingly. A $500,000 account should not have more than $100,000-$150,000 in this 20-30% residual position.

Step Three: Set hard stops at specific technical levels.

Stop #1: If SPCX closes below $140 on the day, exit another 50% of the remaining position. Stop #2: If SPCX closes below $130, exit the rest.

These aren't emotional stops; they're technical levels where the deterioration accelerates. Once you break through $140, the next floor is the lower Bollinger Band at $131.65. If that breaks, the unwind could be severe. Don't hold hoping for a bounce below $130.

Step Four: Monitor the unlock like a hawk, but don't panic pre-emptively.

The aggressive analyst is right that the unlock is a catalyst. But the conservative analyst is also right that if insiders don't actually sell in meaningful volume, that's actually bullish. Watch the first 3-5 trading days after the unlock. If the stock holds up or rallies, that tells you something. If it collapses, exit your remaining 20-30% position immediately. But don't sell your whole position this week assuming the unlock will be catastrophic. That's market timing on an uncertain catalyst.

Step Five: Revisit in 90 days with fresh eyes.

If the company announces major government contracts, successfully tests Starship, or shows any evidence that profitability is coming sooner than expected, you're still in the game with your 20-30% position. If none of that happens and the burn continues, your remaining position is small enough that it's not a portfolio killer if it goes to zero.

Why This Approach Beats Both Recommendations¶

The aggressive analyst wants you to sell 100% into a bounce with high confidence that the market will deteriorate further. That's a strong bet. If you're wrong and the bounce becomes a sustained recovery, you've locked in losses and missed the reversal. That's real risk.

The conservative analyst wants you to hold 70-80% of your position and take a disciplined approach to reduction. That's more prudent than aggressive, but it's too generous to the downside risk. If the technical deterioration accelerates or the unlock triggers a cascade, you're still holding a big position into a downtrend. That's also real risk.

My approach says: Take the aggressive risk management action of exiting 60-70% into the bounce, but preserve the conservative optionality of holding a small position. This way:

  • You lock in capital when liquidity is reasonable
  • You reduce portfolio volatility dramatically
  • You're not betting the farm on either outcome
  • You maintain exposure if the unlikely success scenario materializes
  • Your remaining position is small enough that execution risk is manageable
  • You have hard stops that prevent you from slowly watching a position erode from $151 to $100

The Key Insight Both Analysts Are Missing¶

This company is in a binary outcome zone. Either it reaches profitability in the next 1-2 years and the leverage resolves, or it doesn't and the accumulated losses + capital burn creates a refinancing crisis. There's no middle ground where the stock trades sideways at $150 indefinitely.

Given that binary nature, sizing your position accordingly matters more than being "right" about the direction. If you're sizing like this is a long-term hold, you're overweighting the upside scenario. If you're sizing like this is an imminent zero, you're underweighting the real assets and government demand.

A 20-30% residual position sizes like a lottery ticket. That's appropriate for a binary, uncertain outcome. It's neither greedy nor fearful. It's honest about the risk.

My Final Verdict on the Trader's Decision¶

The trader's recommendation to sell is sound from a risk management perspective. But the framing—that this is obvious and that holding anything is reckless—is overstated.

My modification: Sell 60-70% into the bounce aggressively. Hold 20-30% with discipline. This splits the difference between risk reduction and optionality preservation. It's not as exciting as the aggressive analyst's clear conviction, and it's not as protective as holding a larger core position, but it's the honest answer to "what should a risk manager actually do here?"

The worst outcome isn't being wrong about direction; it's being right about direction but having sized so large that the losses are catastrophic. That's what we're actually trying to prevent. I said "the hard stop at $142 protects capital." But the conservative analyst is correct: once you've committed to holding 50% through the unlock, the emotional experience of exiting at $142 after deterioration is worse than the emotional experience of exiting at $151.80 now. The hard stop defines the pain threshold, not the risk management strategy.

The trader said: "Sell 70-80% into bounces toward $160-$175 before the unlock. Complete by the week of July 22-28. Hold 20-30% with discipline."

This is correct because:

  1. It exits material capital before the catalyst. You're not holding through the unlock hoping insider behavior validates your position. You're out before the mechanism that will reduce liquidity materializes.

  4. It has clear decision rules. Hard stop at $140. Reassess after the unlock based on actual company announcements, not data mechanics.

  5. It's psychologically clean. You exit decisively, you move on, you don't create anchoring to a "hold through catalyst" thesis that will test your discipline every day.

(5) The share unlock in ~3 weeks is a known negative supply catalyst arriving when momentum is weakest; exiting before it optimizes execution while liquidity remains adequate.
